<!-- page 115 -->
2 
 
 
Navajo County Hearing Officer Rules of Procedure 
Animal Care & Control and Environmental Health Ordinances and/or Food Code Violations 
 
The following rules will apply in all cases that come before the Hearing Officer for violations of the Animal 
Care & Control and Environmental Health Ordinances and/or Food Code violations. Civil penalties may be 
set in accordance with state law for violations of Animal Care & Control (A.R.S 11-1006) and Environmental 
Health (A.R.S. 36-132.21C). 
 
Definitions 
 
A. “Attorney" means an attorney (lawyer) licensed to practice law in Arizona. 
 
B. “Board” means the Board of Supervisors. 
 
C. “Chairman” means the Chairman of the Board of Supervisors. 
 
D. “Civil Penalties" means the administrative fines that may be imposed on an Owner, in an amount not 
to exceed the statutory criminal fines for conviction of a class 2 misdemeanor. If the Hearing Officer 
enters a Judgment in favor of the Department, the minimum Civil Penalty shall be $100 to cover the 
expenses associated with the enforcement action and the compensation of the Hearing Officer. 
 
E. “Clerk of the Board” means the Clerk of the Board of Supervisors. 
 
F. “Comply" or “Compliance" means correcting a violation by meeting the applicable requirements of the 
Animal Care & Control and Environmental Health Ordinances or Food Code violations. 
 
G. "Department" means the Code Enforcement Division of the Navajo County Public Health Services 
District, which include Animal Care & Control and Environmental Health programs. 
 
H. "Hearing" means the administrative adjudication by the Hearing Officer at which the Owner and the 
County present their cases regarding the Notice of Violation/Citation. 
 
I. “Hearing Officer” means a person appointed by the Board of Supervisors to adjudicate cases related 
to violations of the Animal Care & Control and Environmental Health Ordinances and/or Food Code 
violations. A Hearing Officer may be an employee of Navajo County but shall not be an employee of 
the Code Enforcement Division. 
 
J. “Inspector" means the Animal Care & Control Officer or Environmental Health inspector or other 
employee authorized to inspect property, deliver Notice of Violation/Citations to enforce county 
ordinances, or enforce Arizona Revised Statutes. 
 
K. “Judgment" means the written decision of the Hearing Officer. 
 
L. "Notice of Violation/Citation" means a written complaint alleging a violation of Animal Care & Control 
or Environmental Health Ordinances and/or Food Code violations. 
 
M. "Owner” means the owner of record of a property that is the subject of a Notice of Violation/Citation. If 
there are multiple Owners, the term "Owner" shall refer to all of them, or the owner that is in care of 
said property. 
 
N. “Property” means the recorded location of a specific establishment, or animal(s) of an Owner (A.R.S 
13-1701.3).

<!-- page 116 -->
3 
 
1. Issuing and Serving the Notice of Violation/Citation 
A case is brought before the Hearing Officer for a violation of Animal Care & Control and Environmental 
Health Ordinances or Food Code violations by issuing a Notice of Violation/Citation to the Owner of the 
property on which the violation is located. 
 
The Notice of Violation/Citation shall describe each violation in sufficient detail for the Owner to understand 
the nature of the violation and the section of the ordinance or code that has been violated. A Notice of 
Violation/Citation may include multiple violations. The Notice of Violation/Citation shall also include the date, 
time, and location of the hearing on the violation and a copy of these Hearing Officer Rules of Procedure. 
The Notice of Violation/Citation shall also include a statement that the owner has the right to be represented 
by an attorney, provided that the Owner or the Owners attorney notifies the Hearing Officer of such 
representation in writing at least five days before the hearing. The Notice of Violation/Citation shall be served 
on the Owner by the Inspector or a Deputy Sheriff at least ten days before the hearing. If there are multiple 
Owners residing at different addresses, each address shall be served. If the Inspector is unable to personally 
serve the Notice of Violation/Citation, it may be served as provided for alternative methods of service in the 
Arizona Rules of Civil Procedure (including without limitation service by certified mail, return receipt 
requested). If a Notice of Violation/Citation cannot be served personally, it must be served by an alternative 
method at least 30 days before the hearing. If an Owner attempts to evade service by refusing to claim 
certified mail, a duplicate Notice of Violation/Citation shall be mailed by first-class mail and service shall be 
deemed complete five days after such mailing. 
 
When service is complete, the Inspector shall file a copy of the Notice of Violation/Citation and proof of 
service with the Hearing Officer. 
 
 
2. Amending the Notice of Violation/Citation 
The Hearing Officer may allow the Notice of Violation/Citation to be amended by the Department at any time 
before a judgment is entered in the case, provided that the amendment does not include any additional or 
different violations and the Hearing Officer determines that the rights of the Owner will not be unfairly 
prejudiced by the amendment. Any allowed changes to a Notice of Violation/Citation are considered to have 
been included in the original Notice of Violation/Citation as of the date it was issued. 
 
 
3. Dismissing the Notice of Violation/Citation 
The Hearing Officer will dismiss the Notice of Violation/Citation when a request for voluntary dismissal is 
received from the Department. A request to dismiss a Notice of Violation/Citation may be made on or before 
the date of the hearing. 
 
If the Notice of Violation/Citation is dismissed, the hearing will not be held. The Hearing Officer will notify the 
Owner and the Department that the hearing has been canceled. 
 
Voluntary dismissal of a Notice of Violation/Citation does not preclude the Department from issuing a new 
Notice of Violation/Citation relating to the same conditions. 
 
 
4. Response to the Notice of Violation/Citation; Right to an Attorney 
The Owner may either admit or deny responsibility for the violation by appearing at the hearing or by mailing 
to the Hearing Officer, no later than ten days before the hearing, a short statement signed by the Owner or 
the owner’s attorney admitting or denying responsibility. 
 
If the Owner admits responsibility, either in writing or at the hearing, the hearing will not be held. Instead, 
the Hearing Officer will enter Judgment for the Department and will mail by first-class mail to both the Owner 
and the Department a copy of the Judgment, which shall clearly set forth the violation, the Civil Penalties that 
have been assessed, what the Owner must do to cure the violation, the date by which the Owner must be in 
compliance, and that the Civil Penalties will be suspended if the Owner is in compliance by the stated date. If 
the Owner wishes to have a hearing on the conditions set out in the Judgment, the Owner must request such 
a hearing in writing no later than15 days after the Judgment was mailed.

<!-- page 117 -->
4 
 
If the Owner denies responsibility, either in writing or at the hearing, the hearing will be held on the date 
and time stated in the Notice of Violation/Citation. 
 
If the Owner or the Owner's attorney does not notify the Hearing Officer at least five days before the hearing 
that the Owner will be represented by an attorney, the right to representation by an attorney is waived. 
However, if the Owner can show good cause for failure to timely notify the Hearing Officer, the Hearing 
Officer may reinstate the Owners right to representation by an attorney but may also continue the hearing to 
a later date in order to give the Department additional time to arrange for representation. 
 
In most cases the Department will not be represented by an attorney unless the Owner is represented by an 
attorney. However, the Department may be represented by the County Attorney in other cases with the prior 
permission of the Hearing Officer. 
 
 
5. Subpoenas: Discovery: Disclosure of Evidence 
At the request of either the Owner or the Department, the Hearing Officer may issue a subpoena to any 
person who is 18 years of age or older, requiring that person to attend the hearing and give testimony. A 
person who has been subpoenaed will receive the statutory witness fee for each day of appearance, plus 
mileage at the current Navajo County rate. The party requesting the subpoena pays the witness fee and 
mileage. 
 
No pre-hearing discovery is allowed unless, in the opinion of the Hearing Officer, extraordinary 
circumstances exist. 
 
At the hearing (or before the hearing by mutual agreement), the Owner and the Department will provide to 
each other a list of witnesses, exhibits and written or recorded statements to be used at the hearing. Upon 
request, the Hearing Officer may continue the hearing to a later time or date in order to provide the Owner 
and the Department additional time to inspect each other’s evidence. 
 
6. Postponement of the Hearing 
The Hearing Officer may postpone a hearing for a period not to exceed 30 days if requested to do so by 
either the Owner or the Department, or if the Hearing Officer believes it is in the interests of justice to do so. 
If a hearing is postponed, the Hearing Office will provide notice of the new hearing date to both parties by 
first-class mail. 
 
 
7. Compliance Before the Hearing 
If the violation is corrected before the hearing, the Inspector shall provide to the Hearing Officer a statement 
of compliance. The Hearing Officer will then cancel the hearing and mail by first-class mail written notice to 
both parties that the hearing has been canceled. 
 
 
8. Conduct of the Hearing 
If the Owner has not previously admitted or denied responsibility, the first order of business at the hearing 
shall be the Owner’s plea. 
 
The Arizona Rules of Evidence do not apply in cases coming before the Hearing Officer. Any evidence that is 
offered may be considered if the Hearing Officer believes that the evidence is relevant to the case and not 
unfairly prejudicial. The Department will present its case first, and then the Owner will present his or her 
case. Each party shall be allowed a brief opening and closing statement. Each party may cross-examine the 
other party's witnesses. After initial presentations, each party may present rebuttal witnesses and evidence. 
The Department shall have the burden of proving the violation by a preponderance of the evidence. An 
Owner is responsible for a violation on the Owners property, notwithstanding occupancy by lessees or 
tenants.

<!-- page 118 -->
5 
 
All testimony shall be given under oath or affirmation. 
 
An audio record of the hearing will be made and maintained by the Hearing Officer for a period of one year. 
 
9. Default: Hearing in Absentia 
If the Owner does not respond in writing to the Notice of Violation/Citation and fails to appear at the 
hearing, the Hearing Officer will find the Owner to be in default and will enter Judgment for the Department. 
The Hearing Officer shall mail by first-class mail to both the Owner and the Department a copy of the default 
Judgment, which shall clearly set forth the violation, the Civil Penalties that have been assessed, what the 
Owner must do to cure the violation, the date by which the Owner must be in compliance, and that all but 
$100 of the Civil Penalties will be suspended if the Owner is in compliance by the stated date. The Hearing

If neither the Owner nor the Owner's attorney appears at the hearing after having denied

Owner and the Department a copy of the Judgment, which shall clearly set forth the violation, the Civil 
Penalties that have been assessed, what the Owner must do to cure the violation, the date by which the 
Owner must be in compliance, and that all but $100 of the Civil Penalties will be suspended if the Owner is in 
compliance by the stated date. The Hearing Officer may set aside the Judgment and set a new hearing date 
if the Owner makes such a request in writing no later than 15 days after the Judgment was mailed, and If the 
Hearing Officer believes there is good cause to set aside the Judgment. 
 
If the County fails to appear at any hearing, the Hearing Officer shall dismiss the Notice of 
Violation/Citation. 
 
 
10. Contested Hearing: Determination of Responsibility 
If the Hearing Officer determines following a contested hearing that the Owner is responsible for the 
violation, the Hearing Officer shall enter Judgment for the Department. The Judgment shall clearly set forth 
the violation, the Civil Penalties that have been assessed, what the Owner must do to cure the violation, the

suspended if the Owner is in compliance by the stated date. The Hearing Officer shall mail by first-class mail 
to the Owner and the Department a copy of the Judgment, together with notice of the Owner’s right of appeal 
to the Board. 
 
If the Hearing Officer determines following a contested hearing that the Owner is not responsible for 
the violation, the Hearing Officer shall enter Judgment for the Owner, dismissing the Notice of 
Violation/Citation. 
 
 
11. Failure to Comply with the Judgment: Collection of Civil Penalties 
If the Owner fails to cure the violation by the compliance date stated in the Judgment, the entire amount of

will result in the Department turning collection of the Civil Penalties over to a collection agency. In the case of 
an appeal to the Board of Supervisors, the Civil Penalties will become due and payable as of the compliance 
date stated In the Judgment or the date of the Board's decision on appeal, whichever is later. 
 
 
12. Appeal to the Board of Supervisors 
Both the Owner and the Department have the right to appeal a Judgment of the Hearing Officer to the Board. 
In order to appeal, a written notice of appeal must be filed with the Clerk of the Board no later than 15 days 
after the Judgment was mailed.

<!-- page 119 -->
6 
 
When the Clerk of the Board receives the notice of appeal, notice will be sent to the Owner and the 
Department, stating the date, time, and location of the Board's review of the Hearing Officers decision and 
the date by which any appeal memoranda must be received. An appeal memorandum is not mandatory,

When a Judgment of the Hearing Officer in favor of the Department has become final by virtue of the Owners

Services District until the violation has been cured and all Civil Penalties have been paid in full, at which time 
the Department shall record a notice that the Judgment has been satisfied. 
 
Any digital evidence on an external drive will not be allowed to be used on any county devices. All external 
USB thumb/flash drives, memory sticks/cards/discs, and camera flash cards that are not the property 
of Navajo County are PROHIBITED on county devices. 
 
 
14. Limitation on Number of Notice of Violation/Citation 
The Department shall not issue and prosecute more than two Notice of Violation/Citation in regard to the

Meeting Date: 01/23/2024  
Title: Fatality Review Annual Reports
Submitted For: Janelle Linn, Health Director 
Submitted By: Alyssa Lemmon, Public Health Nurse PT-PRN
Department: NC Public Health Services District
Presented By: Alyssa Lemmon
Subject:
Presentation to inform on most recent Fatality Review data
Background: 
NCPHSD conducts three different fatality reviews:
Child Fatality Review-we review Navajo and Apache County child deaths for 10+ years. 
Overdose Fatality Review-we currently review Navajo County Overdose deaths, however we
do not include deaths occurring on tribal lands or where primary residence is on tribal lands due
to insufficient records received from tribal agencies to facilitate effective reviews. Since 2019.
Suicide Mortality Review- we currently review Navajo County Suicide deaths, however we do
not include deaths occurring on tribal lands or where primary residence is on tribal lands due to
insufficient records received from tribal agencies to facilitate effective reviews. Since 2021.

<!-- page 121 -->
Child Fatality Review
Annual Report
2022

<!-- page 122 -->
Introduction
According to the National Center for Fatality
Review and Prevention, every year almost
37,000 children in the United States will die
before their 18th birthday. In the state of
Arizona, 863 children died in 2021, which is
an increase from 2020, in which 838
children died.  Under the direction and with
the assistance of The Arizona Department
of Health Services (ADHS), Navajo County
Public Health Services District (NCPHSD)
holds local Child Fatality Review (CFR) team
meetings.
Background
The purpose of the Child Fatality Review is to
conduct a multidisciplinary, in-depth review of
child deaths. This process leads to a better
understanding of how and why children die,
the identification of risk and protective
factors, and the implementation of
community-specific prevention measures and
strategies to reduce and eliminate child
deaths (data.ncfrp.org). Discussing the
findings from these review meetings guides
local programs and the revision and
development of services and policies to keep
infants and children in our communities safe
and healthy.

multidisciplinary team, including
generation of recommendations.

<!-- page 124 -->
While reviewing data on each child fatality, multiple commonalities were noted to be shared
among the children who died. Recognizing commonalities among decedents provides insight
into risk factors and highlights opportunities for prevention activities.
Major Themes
53% listed child abuse, neglect, poor supervision, and/or exposure to hazards as a
contributing factor to the child’s death. (Hazards include untreated maternal sexually
transmitted infections, no prenatal care, unsafe gun storage, unhealthy living situation,
use of harmful substances near children, and unsafe sleep environment.)
45% of all infant deaths received late prenatal care (care started at six months gestation
or later) or no prenatal care.
36% of infant deaths had intrauterine alcohol exposure.
55% of infant deaths were born drug exposed.
Our CFR team determined that 63% of child deaths probably could have been prevented.
Of these possibly preventable deaths, 42% were suicides, 25% were natural, 17% were
accidental, 8% were homicides, and 8% were undetermined. 
The team determined that 26% of child deaths in Navajo County probably could not have
been prevented (all child deaths that were determined by the team that were probably not
preventable were of natural causes).
17% of preventable deaths had a delay in CPR initiation until EMS arrival and could have
possibly benefitted from caregiver CPR education.

*Definitions
Homicide: According to
Merriam-Webster, the
definition of a homicide is “the
killing of one human being by
another. 
Suicide: The definition of a
suicide is “the act or instance
of taking one’s own life
voluntarily and intentionally.” 
Accidental death: An accidental
death is “one that {occurs} as
the unforeseen and chance
result of an intended act.”
(examples of an accidentally
death include a motor vehicle
accident, fire, burns,
electrocution, drowning,
asphyxia, bodily force or
weapon, fall or crush,
poisoning, overdose, acute
intoxication, etc.) 
Natural death: A natural death
occurs “in the course of nature
and from natural causes (as
age or disease) as opposed to
accident or violence”. (ex:
congenital anomaly,
prematurity, infection, low birth
weight, cancer, an injury,
neurological disorder, diabetes,
or another medical condition.)
Undetermined/Unknown:
Undetermined and Unknown
deaths are those in which there
was not enough information to
be able to say for certain what
the manner of death was.

<!-- page 130 -->
Conclusion
In 2022, the Navajo County Child Fatality Review team determined that 63% of the child
deaths that occurred likely could have been prevented. Prevention measures that have
been identified include education (pregnancy-related, for parents, schools and community)
and utilization of appropriate services.
The Native American population was disproportionately represented in the amount of child
deaths in our county in 2022. While Native Americans make up 43.5% of the population of
Navajo County, they represented 63% of all child deaths in 2022. This significant
discrepancy suggests that our prevention measures are not adequately reaching this
demographic in our population and improvements are necessary. 
The over-arching goal of our multi-disciplinary CFR team is to aid our community in
preventing future child deaths. We accomplish this goal by completing an in-depth case
review of each child death in Navajo County to discuss risk factors, protective factors, and
actions that our community can implement to enhance the preventability of further deaths.

<!-- page 132 -->
Adverse Childhood Experiences
Adverse childhood experiences, or ACES are potentially traumatic events that occur in
childhood (0-17 years). ACEs include but are not limited to experiencing or witnessing
violence, abuse, or neglect; having a family member attempt or die by suicide. Also included
are aspects of the child’s environment that can undermine their sense of safety, stability, and
bonding such as growing up in a household with substance misuse, mental health problems,
or instability due to parental separation or incarceration of a parent, sibling or other member
of the household, or having a lack of basics like food and housing.
According to the CDC, research indicates that toxic stress, from ACEs, during childhood can
harm the most basic levels of the nervous, endocrine, and immune systems, and that such
exposures can even alter the physical structure of DNA. Changes to the brain from toxic
stress can affect such things as attention, impulsive behavior, decision-making, learning,
emotion, and response to stress. Children growing up under toxic conditions can struggle to
learn and complete schooling, are at increased risk of becoming involved in crime and
violence, using alcohol or drugs, and engaging in other health-risk behaviors (e.g., early
initiation of sexual activity; unprotected sex; and suicide attempts). They are susceptible to
disease, illness, and mental health challenges over their lifetime, often contributing to their
early death.
ACEs are sometimes revealed in medical and mental health records, though most ACES are
not reported. 
Research reports there are several strategies that can prevent ACEs from happening in the
first place, as well as strategies to mitigate the harms of ACEs. The evidence tells us that
ACEs can be prevented by:
• Strengthening economic supports for families
• Promoting social norms that protect against violence and adversity
• Ensuring a strong start for children and paving the way for them to reach their full potential
• Teaching skills to help parents and youth handle stress, manage emotions, and tackle
everyday challenges
• Connecting youth to caring adults and activities
• Intervening to lessen immediate and long-term harms
For more information on ACEs, visit:
https://www.cdc.gov/violenceprevention/aces/index.html
https://www.cdc.gov/violenceprevention/pdf/preventingACES.pdf
https://developingchild.harvard.edu/resources/aces-and-toxic-stress-frequently-asked-
questions/

<!-- page 136 -->
Introduction
According to the Centers for Disease
Control and Prevention (CDC), there were
an estimated 107,622 drug overdose
deaths in the United States during 2021;
an approximate 15% increase from 2020.
Deaths from drug overdose have become
the leading cause of injury-related death
in the United States. Arizona experienced
2,015 opioid-related overdose deaths in
2021. Under the direction of and with the
assistance of The Arizona Department of
Health Services (ADHS), Navajo County
Public Health Services District (NCPHSD)
commenced its first Overdose Fatality
Review (OFR) team meeting in January of
2020.
Background
The overarching goal of the OFR is to
eliminate deaths  related to substance
misuse. This is accomplished by reviewing
overdose fatalities (“decedents”),
identifying system gaps, and community-
specific overdose prevention and
intervention strategies. The OFR team is a
multidisciplinary group of volunteers that
participate in the confidential,
comprehensive death reviews. As
community stakeholders, agencies, and
residents employ evidence-based and
innovative interventions, we can empower
our community to contend against the
opioid epidemic, save lives, and promote
healing among individuals and families.
Objectives
3. Create Case Summaries
4. Review of cases performed by
multidisciplinary team, including
generation of recommendations.
Data Sources
The ADHS Office of Injury Prevention and
Violence Prevention provides county level
drug-related death data from the
Database Application of Vital Events
(D.A.V.E.). Under local authority, NCPHSD
collects data from the Navajo County
Office of the Medical Examiner, the Navajo
County Office of Vital Records, Arizona
Department of Corrections, local law
enforcement and emergency medical
services records, behavioral health and
medical records (when applicable), and
family interviews. Inclusion and exclusion
criteria* for deaths reviewed were
determined to reduce duplicative efforts
and to provide the most informed reviews
based on available records. 
Methodology of Case Reviews
1.  Identify Cases 
2. Obtain Records
*For inclusion and exclusion criteria, see page 8

Recommendations
The Navajo County Overdose Fatality Review Team identified key prevention recommendations
for each fatality reviewed. Recommendations were categorized into four main areas, including
Healthcare, Criminal Justice, Crisis Response, and Community/Public Health. The
recommendations below were the most frequently recognized from each category and were
shared with a variety of community partners to improve and enhance services in Navajo County: 
Improve access to primary care services,
to include 
substance use disorder screening 
harm reduction education
medication-assisted treatment (MAT) 
referral to treatment
Healthcare
Establish protocols for emergency
department (ED) and inpatient hospital
discharge instructions, to include
naloxone (Narcan) distribution
connection to peer recovery
specialists 
case management
treatment services
Increase patient-specific education of
overdose risk associated with age,
comorbid conditions, prescription
medications, and the use of other
substances
Improve care coordination post-release
by performing a warm hand off to 
substance use disorder treatment 
medical care 
mental health services 
as well as providing social support
resources (housing, employment,
insurance, transportation, etc.)
Criminal Justice
Enhance universal training on
evidence-based overdose prevention
for the first responder community
(EMS, Fire, Law Enforcement, etc.)
Crisis Response
Improve support for families who have
a loved one with a substance use
disorder (e.g., community events,
connection to support groups, written
materials)
Community/Public Health
Improve support for individuals with
past childhood or adult trauma (e.g.,
domestic violence, sexual assault, loss
of a loved one)
Increase education and awareness of
overdose prevention and harm
reduction tools and strategies (e.g.,
naloxone, fentanyl test strips, HIV/HEP
C testing)
Improve access to social support
resources such as housing,
employment, insurance,
transportation, etc.

Summary of Data
Toxicology results indicated that 69% of deaths included 3 or more substances. Any given overdose fatality
may include 1 to 6 substances present at the time of death. Other substances found in toxicology testing
included: Antihistamines (Diphenhydramine, hydroxyzine), gabapentin, Antidepressants (fluoxetine,
citalopram, trazodone, mirtazapine) Sedatives (clonazepam, diazepam, temazepam), Non-opioid
analgesic/Anti-inflammatory medication (mitragynine [kratom], ibuprofen, acetaminophen), Anxiolytics
(buspirone), and Decongestants (pseudoephedrine). 
Limitations: While extensive efforts are made to acquire thorough records from multiple cross-sector entities
and conduct next of kin interviews on each decedent, the information is often not available. When this occurs,
the default for answering required data fields is “No.” This can result in data being inconsistent with what may
have been true for the decedent. As a result, the percentages listed in this report are considered to be low.

Suicide Mortality Review
Annual Report
2021-2022
Talk with us, there is hope. Call 988

<!-- page 152 -->
Introduction
The Centers for Disease Control and
Prevention report that 48,183 people died
by suicide in the United States during 2021.
Around 12 million adults seriously thought
about suicide, 3.5 million adults made a
plan, and 1.7 million adults attempted
suicide. According to the Arizona
Department of Health Services (ADHS),
Arizona’s rate of suicide was 35 percent
higher than the national rate, with suicide
being the 10th leading cause of death in the
state. On March 30, 2020, Jake’s Law was
signed by Governor Ducey to address
mental health in Arizona, including the
establishment of state and local Suicide
Mortality Review (SMR) teams.
Background
The overarching goal of the SMR team is to
identify community- and systems-related
circumstances that influence suicide death.
The SMR team is a multidisciplinary group of
volunteers that participate in confidential,
comprehensive death reviews.
The objectives during a review encompass: 
identifying barriers and service system
issues
identifying risk factors and trends in
suicides
identifying protective factors to decrease
suicide risk
identifying needed changes in legislation,
policies, training, and services to
implement prevention activities
improving the delivery of services to
individuals, families, and communities
educating the public about the incidences
and causes of suicide and the public’s role
in preventing suicide deaths
Objectives
3. Create Case Summaries
4. Review of cases performed by
multidisciplinary team, including
generation of recommendations.
Data Sources
The ADHS Office of Injury Prevention and
Violence Prevention provides county level
suicide-related death data from the
Database Application of Vital Events. Under
local authority, Navajo County Public Health
Services District (NCPHSD) collects data
from the Navajo County Office of the Medical
Examiner, the Navajo County Office of Vital
Records, Arizona Department of Corrections,
local law enforcement records, emergency
medical services records, behavioral health
records, medical records (when applicable),
and family interviews. Inclusion and
exclusion criteria for deaths reviewed were
determined to reduce duplicative efforts and
to provide the most informed reviews, based
on available records.
Methodology of Case Reviews
1. Identify Cases 
2. Obtain Records
*For inclusion and exclusion criteria, see page 6

<!-- page 154 -->
While reviewing data on each suicide
mortality, multiple commonalities were
identified among the individuals who died.
These commonalities can include similar
events, traits, life circumstances, health
status, and more. Though not classified as
causal factors, identifying commonalities
among suicide mortality decedents
provides insight into risk factors and
highlights opportunities for intervention.
Major Themes
According to research, ACEs have been
linked to chronic health conditions,
mental illness, and substance use in
adulthood. ACEs are sometimes
revealed in medical/mental health
records. However, the history of
childhood trauma is more often
disclosed during the family/next of kin
interview. While family interviews are
attempted for each decedent, 55% of
the cases from 2021 were lacking this
significant contribution. 
*An ACE score of 4 or more doubles or triples the
risk of serious psychological and chronic health
issues.
61% had some form of crisis within two
weeks of death
50% had expressed suicidal feelings,
thoughts, intentions, or plans—either
directly or indirectly—within one
month of death
22% left a suicide note
17% had a veteran status
33% had alcohol consumption (either
on toxicology or reported/witnessed
on scene) prior to death
50% had reported or demonstrated
signs or symptoms of behavioral
health challenges within 30 days of
death
56% had at least one chronic health
condition
28% had a history of chronic pain
ACEs include, but are not limited to:
experiencing or witnessing violence,
abuse, or neglect
having a family member die by suicide
growing up in an environment/household
with substance use or mental health
problems
separation from a parent by divorce or
incarceration
67% had experienced at least one
Adverse Childhood Experience (ACE)
33% experienced four or more ACEs*
and other-unspecified (exposure to
community violence; victim of bullying,
physical or verbal; refugee or wartime
experience; victim of natural disaster;
witnessing or experiencing acts of
terrorism)
Talk with us, there is hope. Call 988

<!-- page 155 -->
Recommendations and 
Actions Taken
The Navajo County Suicide Mortality Review team identified key prevention recommendations
for each fatality reviewed. Recommendations are categorized into four main areas: Healthcare,
Criminal Justice, Crisis Response, and Community/Public Health. The recommendations below
were the most frequently recognized from each category and were shared with a variety of
community partners to improve and enhance services in Navajo County.
Improve access to appropriate mental
healthcare services
Increase training for healthcare staff to
better screen and treat depression
Increase clinical community awareness of
support organizations to assist during the
immediate crisis and into recovery
Healthcare
Improve care coordination post-release
(warm hand-off to alcohol/substance
use treatment, medical care, and
community-based mental health
services)
Increase training for corrections health
and custody staff on evidence-based
suicide and self-harm prevention 
Criminal Justice
Improve support for individuals with
past childhood or adult trauma
(domestic violence, sexual assault,
loss of a loved one, etc.)
Certified staff as ACEs trainers
through Arizona ACEs Consortium
Provided ACEs training to Navajo
County Courts staff
Community/Public Health
Increase access to appropriate mental
health/substance use services (such as
transport or referral to treatment)
Increase first responder (EMS, Fire, Law
Enforcement, etc.) training on evidence-
based suicide and self-harm prevention
Increase number of crisis response teams
Crisis Response
Improve social connectedness
Decrease stigma and increase help-
seeking behavior
Staff attended community
outreach events to spread
awareness about mental health
resources and education 
Staff participated in ADHS Suicide
Prevention Workgroups
Staff attended 114th Arizona Town
Hall: “Mental Health, Substance
Use, and Homelessness”
Talk with us, there is hope. Call 988

<!-- page 156 -->
Increase gatekeeper training for community members to identify people who may be
at risk of suicide and how to respond effectively
Certified staff as Adult and Youth Mental Health First Aid (MHFA) trainers
In 2021, Navajo County certified 16 adults in Adult MHFA and 48 adults in Youth
MHFA (Northland Pioneer College, Sequoia Village Schools, NCPHSD)
In 2022, Navajo County certified 66 adults in Adult MHFA and 29 adults in Youth
MHFA (NCPHSD, Navajo County Probation, Winslow High School, Copperstate
Farms)
Certified staff as Question, Persuade, and Refer Gatekeeper Trainers
Community/Public Health continued
Reduce access to lethal means by people who are at acute risk of suicide (safe
storage of medications, firearms, and other household products)
Navajo County Outreach liaisons acquired free gun locks to provide to community
members
Locks were also provided to law enforcement agencies, Show Low VA Clinic, and
other institutions. 
More locks are available from Navajo County Public Health
Recommendations and 
Actions Taken (continued)
Increase education and awareness of population-based resources for mental health
and wellness
Promoted resources and education through the Navajo County Community
Council on Mental Health 
Provided a mental health resource guide for community members in Navajo
County
Compiled and distributed comprehensive Veteran’s Resource Guide pamphlets
throughout community

<!-- page 169 -->
Purpose
• Many deaths are preventable
• Comprehensive, multidisciplinary review of deaths
(People with Lived Experience/Recovery, Law Enforcement, Public Health, Behavioral Health, EMS, 
ME, Hospital Administration, County Attorney)
❖To understand how and why preventable deaths happen
❖To identify system gaps, missed opportunities for prevention and 
intervention
❖To identify community-specific prevention strategies
❖To identify risk and protective factors

<!-- page 170 -->
Child Fatality Review (CFR) 2022 deaths
19 Navajo County children
•63% of deaths likely 
could have been 
prevented. 
•63% Native American
•58% less than one year 
old
•55% of infant deaths 
were born drug exposed
•36% of infant death had 
intrauterine alcohol 
exposure
•26% Suicide

<!-- page 171 -->
CFR Recommendations
PREGNANCY
• Early prenatal care
• Risk of substance use & treatment options
• STI treatment during pregnancy
PARENTING
• Earlier recognition of suicide warning signs
• Available mental health services for children
• Educate parents on how to be more engaged 
with and how to talk with children/teens
• Gun storage/safety education
• CPR/First aid education
SCHOOLS/COMMUNITY
• When to report abuse/neglect to DCS
• Increased use of counseling 
• Education for teens about suicide warning 
signs and what to do
• Decrease stigma by healthcare workers 
regarding SUD in pregnant population
SERVICE UTILIZATION
• Peer Support for youth
• Appropriate referrals for domestic violence 
victims
• Earlier family involvement with social services
• Family psycho- therapy
• Improved continuity of care after inpatient 
psychiatric discharge

<!-- page 172 -->
ACTIVITIES & SERVICES
• Car Seat and Safe Sleep Education
• Safety Town
• Mental Health First Aid (for adults)
• Youth Mental Health First Aid (for adults who work with youth)
• Creating Safe Spaces (mental health for youth)
• Navajo County Teen Health Program

or adult trauma

CRIMINAL JUSTICE

referral to treatment

❖ Narcan distribution, connection to Peer, treatment 
services

<!-- page 180 -->
COMMUNITY CONCERNS
Community Health Assessment Survey, 2023
• 42% responded they had anxiety and/or depression.  
❖9.7% suicide attempt/thoughts and self-harm
• Substance use disorder is a primary concern of residents
❖Alcohol use disorder #1 in all Tribal regions
❖Individuals with SUD in top 3 social groups whose healthcare needs are not met at all 
❖Top 3 needed services not received at release of incarceration
– Peer support services
– Mental health treatment
– MAT
• Lack of mental health services/access to services
❖ 32% said lack of access to mental/behavioral health services is a top barrier to 
healthcare in the community
❖ Mental Health conditions was reported as #1 services needed but not received

PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 03-2024, 
approving/denying a Special Use Permit to allow for the construction of a 105’ monopole
communication tower and associated facilities located north of the Winslow area.
Background:
 APN#: 103-23-010A, T19N, R16E, S06 of the GSRM. Owner: Leonard
Lucero. Agent: Graham Chapman.
This proposal would allow for one (1) 105’ monopole wireless communication facility and
associated facilities on a vacant, 12.43-acre lot. The proposal would be an appropriate usage
within the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance, if approved for a Special Use Permit. The Navajo
County Planning and Zoning Commission unanimously voted to recommend approval of the
Special Use Permit on December 14th, 2023. 
Recommendation: 
The Navajo County Planning and Development Staff recommend approval of the Special Use
Permit for AZ11-173 Huntress Wireless Communication Facility, subject to the conditions
listed in the Staff Report. The Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission unanimously
voted to recommend approval of the Special Use Permit, subject to the conditions listed in the
Staff Report, on December 14th, 2023.

NAVAJO COUNTY PUBLIC WORKS DEPARTMENT 
PLANNING & DEVELOPMENT 
 
 
STAFF REPORT  
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
 
Hearing Date: January 23rd, 2024 
Case No:  SUP 23-019 
Action:   Special Use Permit  
Applicant/Owner: Pinnacle Consulting, Inc./Leonard Lucero 
Project Name: AZ11-173 HUNTRESS WIRELESS COMMUNICATION FACILITY 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
Recommendation: Staff recommends approval of the Special Use Permit, subject to the conditions of 
this Staff Report. The Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission unanimously voted to 
recommend approval of the Special Use Permit on December 14th, 2023.  
 
Location: The property is located north of Winslow, on APN# 103-23-010A 
 
Size: One (1) parcel totaling 12.43 acres.  
 
Supervisor District: II. 
 
Zoning: A-General. 
 
Property Owners: Leonard Lucero. 
 
Applicant: Pinnacle Consulting, Inc.  
 
Representative: Graham Chapman.  
 
REQUEST DESCRIPTION:  
Consideration of a request by Pinnacle Consulting for a Special Use Permit to allow for the construction 
of a 105’ monopole communication tower and associated facilities on a 12.43-acre parcel located north 
of Winslow. APN#: 103-23-010A, T19N, R16E, S06 of the GSRM. Owner: Leonard Lucero. Agent: 
Graham Chapman. 
 
GENERAL CHARACTER OF NEIGHBORHOOD:  
 
The majority of land in this area is zoned A -General, with the Ames Acres and Five Mountain Estates 
subdivisions located to the west. The Winslow Wastewater Plant sits just under a mile and a half to the

<!-- page 186 -->
Board of Supervisors             2 | P a g e  
 
south, the Winslow Levee borders this parcel from the east, and there is vacant land to the north.  
 
BACKGROUND/HISTORY: 
 
This proposal would allow for one (1) 105’ monopole wireless communication facility and associated 
facilities on a vacant, 12.43-acre lot. The proposal would be an appropriate usage within the Navajo 
County Zoning Ordinance, if approved for a Special Use Permit. 
 
At the December 14 th, 2023 meeting, the Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission 
unanimously voted to recommend approval of the Special Use Permit to the Navajo County Board of 
Supervisors, subject to the conditions listed in this Report.  
 
PROJECT DESCRIPTION:  
 
The proposed project is a 105’ monopole wireless communication facility and its associated facilities. 
This tower will be located at least 107ft. away from all property lines on APN# 103-23-010A. The tower 
and all associated equipment will be located in a 40’x40’ compound, surrounded by a 10ft. chain-link 
fence. Additionally, the equipment platform will be elevated 5’ above ground level.  This compound 
will be sitting on a leased, 585’x215’ portion of APN#103-23-010A.  
 
ZONING DISTRICT: 
ARTICLE 3 – (A-GENERAL) – GENERAL ZONING DISTRICT 
 
Section 301 – Purpose  
 
The main purpose of this A-General Zone is to provide for all the unincorporated area  of the County, except 
land within the boundary of a reservation and outside the authority of Navajo County not otherwise designated 
for some other specific zone, to be included in the "A-General Zone", by this Ordinance. No subdividing shall 
be conducted or approved in the "A -General Zone", without prior re -zoning of the land so  intended. Uses 
permitted in the "A -General Zone” include farm and non -farm residential uses, fa rms, recreational, 
institutional, commercial, and industrial uses as specifically listed in this Article. Other uses may be permitted 
as Special Uses under Article 20. 
 
ZONING ORDINANCE: 
Article 20 – Special Uses and Planned Unit Developments 
 
Section 2001 – Special Uses 
  
The Board of Supervisors may permit as a Special Use the following uses in zoning districts 
from which they are otherwise prohibited by this Ordinance, unless any such use is further 
prohibited by this Article. 
 
 34. Television, wireless towers, and radio transmitter towers and stations, subject  
 to approval by Federal Regulatory Agencies. 
 
Article 25 – General Provisions

<!-- page 187 -->
Board of Supervisors             3 | P a g e  
 
 
Section 2521 – Wireless Communication Facilities 
  
The purpose of this Section is to establish rules and regulations for the siting of wireless 
communication facilities. The goals of this Section are to provide for the development of 
wireless communication services County-wide by:  
 
1. Considering public safety, health, and welfare.  
 
2. Considering impact on adjacent properties. 
 
3. Encouraging configuration which minimizes additional visual impact through proper 
siting, design, landscape, and camouflage techniques.  
 
COMPREHENSIVE PLAN/AREA PLAN:  
Comprehensive Plan 
 
Land Use Element 
 
Goal 6: Maintain compatible land use patterns while encouraging free market development. 
 
Policy 6-B: Consider, if needed, providing for buffers between different land uses including, but 
not limited to, setbacks, walls or fences, and landscaping.  
 
• The applicant has indicated they will set back their wireless communication tower a 
minimum of 107’ from all property lines, which exceeds the requirements of the Navajo 
County Zoning Ordinance for facilities of this nature.  Additionally, the applicant has 
indicated they will construct a 10ft. chain -link fence  around the perimeter of the 
compound area, acting as a buffer between the proposed tower and adjacent properties.  
 
Goal 9: Actively plan to accommodate growth in Navajo County in an appropriate manner. 
 
Policy 9-F: Encourage development/installation of technology improvements including, but not 
limited to, high speed Internet lines and other communications improvements in Navajo County. 
 
• The applicant is proposing the addition of this wireless communication tower and 
associated facilities to provide higher quality wireless service for residents, businesses, 
visitors, and emergency services in this region of Navajo County. 
 
Analysis: 
 
The proposed special use meets all relevant goals and policies of the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance 
and the Navajo County  Comprehensive Plan as described above. This project, and its submittal, meet 
all requirements of Section 2521 of the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance, which regulates wireless 
communication facilities.  
 
Planning and Development Services Staff have  received no public comment s for the project as of

<!-- page 188 -->
Board of Supervisors             4 | P a g e  
 
January 11th, 2023. 
 
FINDINGS OF FACT: 
 
1. The item has been properly noticed and posted in compliance with Arizona Revised Statutes 
and Article 29.  
 
This item was posted in the January 3rd, 2024, edition of the Holbrook Tribune.  Postcards were 
mailed to neighbors within 300 feet of the property. The property was posted with public hearing 
notices, in accordance with the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance and Arizona Revised Statutes.  
 
2. The proposal is in compliance with the Comprehensive Plan. 
 
The proposed project meets a ll applicable goals within Navajo County’s Comprehensive Plan, as 
noted within this report. 
 
3. The proposed use is compatible with the permissible uses in the zoning district in which the 
property is located. 
 
The proposed use is compatible with permissible uses within the A-General zoning district. 
 
4. The proposed use is  compatible with the current and likely future uses of properties in the 
vicinity of the proposed use.  
 
The proposed use has been determined to be compatible with current and likely future uses per the 
Navajo County Zoning Ordinance.  
 
5. The proposed use will have minimal adverse effects on the public health, safety, and general 
welfare of properties in the vicinity of the proposed use. 
 
Given the nature of the property and the zoning of the ne ighborhood, there should be minimal 
adverse effects on the public health, safety, and general welfare of properties in the vicinity of the 
proposed use.

PLANNING AND DEVELOPMENT SERVICES RECOMMENDATION:  
 
The  Navajo County Planning and Development Services Staff recommend approval of the Special Use Permit, 
subject to the following conditions: 
 
1. This Special Use Permit shall permit 1 (one)  monopole wireless communication tower, not to 
exceed 105 feet in height, and accessory structures necessary for operation of the tower on the 
subject property. 
 
2. The applicant shall obtain a Building Permit from Navajo County for such tower and facilities 
prior to commencing construction. 
 
3. The applicant shall meet all State and Federal requirements concerning communication towers 
prior to any building permits being issued. 
 
4. The tower shall be constructed as a monopole, as indicated in the supplied plans.  The owner of 
the tower shall maintain the tower, antennae as necessary. 
 
5. A fence of material acceptable to the Planning and Development Services Director shall be constructed 
to enclose the base of the tower and necessary accessory structures.  
 
6. A Geotechnical Report for the Access Easement shall be submitted and approved. 
 
7. The permitted Special Use shall be allowed to occur per the attached/approved site plan.  Any 
expansion or change of the Special Use shall require an amendment to this permit. 
 
8. If the permitted Special Use is not utilized within a twelve (12) month period, from the date of 
Board approval, this permit shall become null and void. 
 
9. Should the operator decide to abandon the use of the tower, they shall notify Navajo County 
Planning and Development Services that the tower is no longer being utilized. 
 
10. If the tower is no longer being utilized, the property owner shall cause, at their expense, the 
removal of all components of this tower within a 90-day period. 
 
11. The owner shall provide reasonable cooperation to other public and private users who may be 
interested in collocation on this tower and shall consider such collocation upon commercially 
reasonable terms, unless such collocation is not technically feasible. 
 
12. This Special Use Permit shall be personal to the permittee and applicable only to the specific use and 
to the specific property for which it is issued. Upon completion and final inspection by the Planning 
and Development Services Director (or designee) of any authorized structures, signifying that all 
zoning and site development requirements imposed in connection with the permit have been satisfied, 
the Special Use Permit shall thereafter be transferable and shall run with the land, whereupon the 
maintenance of special conditions imposed by the permit, as well as the compliance with other 
provisions of this title, shall be the responsibility of the property owner. 
 
13. This Special Use Permit and the subject site shall be reviewed and inspected by Planning and

<!-- page 190 -->
Board of Supervisors             6 | P a g e  
 
Development Staff on the fifth (5 th) anniversary of Board of Supervisors approval and every five (5) 
years thereafter to ensure the site is being utilized as approved and is in compliance with all conditions 
contained herein.  If the subject site is found to be out of compliance with these conditions, this Special 
Use Permit and its privileges may be revoked.

AAS
ALTHOUGH RARE, VIBRATIONS SEVERE ENOUGH TO CAUSE DAMAGE
CAN OCCASIONALLY OCCUR IN STRUCTURES OF ALL TYPES.
BECAUSE THEY ARE INFLUENCED BY MANY INTERACTING
VARIABLES, VIBRATIONS ARE GENERALLY UNPREDICTABLE.  THE
USER'S MAINTENANCE PROGRAM SHOULD INCLUDE OBSERVATION
FOR EXCESSIVE VIBRATION AND EXAMINATION FOR ANY
STRUCTURAL DAMAGE OR BOLT LOOSENING.  THE LIMITATIONS OF
THIS DESIGN SPECIFICALLY EXCLUDE FATIGUE FAILURE OR SIMILAR
PHENOMENA RESULTING FROM INDUCED VIBRATION, HARMONIC
OSCILLATION OR RESONANCE ASSOCIATED WITH MOVEMENT OF
AIR CURRENTS AROUND THE PRODUCT.

NOTES & SPECIFICATIONS1
SPECIAL INSPECTIONS:
1. STEEL FABRICATION SHALL BE DONE ON THE PREMISES OF A
FABRICATOR REGISTERED AND APPROVED AS REQUIRED BY THE
BUILDING OFFICIAL TO PERFORM SUCH WORK WITHOUT SPECIAL
INSPECTION. ALTERNATIVELY, SPECIAL INSPECTION OF MATERIALS,
WELDING, AND FABRICATION PROCEDURES SHALL BE REQUIRED FOR
FABRICATION BY AN UNAPPROVED FABRICATOR.
2. NO FIELD WELDING SHALL BE PERMITTED
3. THE FOLLOWING SPECIAL INSPECTIONS SHALL BE REQUIRED PER
CHAPTER 17 OF THE BUILDING CODE:
·PERIODIC SPECIAL INSPECTION OF HIGH-STRENGTH BOLTING (WHEN
APPLICABLE)
·PERIODIC SPECIAL INSPECTION OF PLACEMENT OF REINFORCING STEEL
·CONTINUOUS SPECIAL INSPECTION OF ANCHOR BOLTS PRIOR TO AND
DURING CONCRETE PLACEMENT
·CONTINUOUS SPECIAL INSPECTION OF CONCRETE PLACEMENT
·CONTINUOUS SPECIAL INSPECTION OF DRILLING OPERATIONS FOR PIER
FOUNDATIONS
·CONTINUOUS SPECIAL INSPECTION TO VERIFY LOCATION, PLUMBNESS,
DIAMETER, AND LENGTH OF PIER FOUNDATIONS
4. SPECIAL INSPECTION IS NOT REQUIRED FOR WORK OF A MINOR NATURE
OR AS WARRANTED BY CONDITIONS IN THE JURISDICTION AS APPROVED
BY THE BUILDING OFFICIAL.  THUS, SPECIAL INSPECTION ITEMS ABOVE
MAY BE WAIVED AS DEEMED APPROPRIATE BY THE BUILDING OFFICIAL.
STRUCTURAL OBSERVATION:
NO STRUCTURAL OBSERVATION IS REQUIRED.
DISCLAIMERS:
1. ALL STRUCTURAL COMPONENTS TO BE CONNECTED TOGETHER SHALL
BE COMPLETELY FIT UP ON THE GROUND OR OTHERWISE VERIFIED FOR
COMPATIBILITY PRIOR TO LIFTING ANY COMPONENT INTO PLACE.
REPAIRS REQUIRED DUE TO FIT-UP OR CONNECTION COMPATIBILITY
PROBLEMS AFTER PARTIAL ERECTION ARE THE FINANCIAL
RESPONSIBILITY OF THE CONTRACTOR.
2. WHERE EFFECTIVE PROJECTED AREAS (EPA) ARE USED, IT IS THE
RESPONSIBILITY OF OTHERS TO VERIFY INSTALLED EQUIPMENT DOES
NOT EXCEED LISTED EPA.

GENERAL DESIGN NOTES:
STRUCTURAL DESIGN IS BASED ON THE 2018 EDITION
INTERNATIONAL BUILDING CODE WITH ALL APPLICABLE
LOCAL AMENDMENTS AND THE TIA-222-H STANDARD.
DESIGN LOADS:
WIND: WIND SPEED = 115 MPH (3-SEC GUST) PER THE ASCE 7-16
STANDARD
RISK CATEGORY / STRUCTURE CLASS: II
EXPOSURE:  C
TOPOGRAPHIC CATEGORY:  1
CREST HEIGHT:  0 FT
ELEVATION:  4830 FT ABOVE SEA LEVEL
ICE: NONE PER THE TIA-222-H STANDARD
SEISMIC:
IMPORTANCE FACTOR:  1.00
RISK CATEGORY / STRUCTURE CLASS: II
MAPPED SPECTRAL RESPONSE ACCELERATIONS:
SS = 0.233g,  S1 = 0.073g
SITE CLASS:  D-DEFAULT
SPECTRAL RESPONSE COEFFICIENTS:
SDS = 0.248g,  SD1 = 0.117g
SEISMIC DESIGN CATEGORY:  B
BASIC SEISMIC-FORCE-RESISTING-SYSTEM:
TELECOMMUNICATION TOWER: STEEL POLE
SEISMIC RESPONSE COEFFICIENT, Cs:  0.030
SEISMIC BASE SHEAR, V:  0.662 K
RESPONSE MODIFICATION FACTOR, R:  1.5
ANALYSIS PROCEDURE:  EQUIVALENT LATERAL FORCE
STRUCTURAL STEEL:
1. POLYGONAL MONOPOLE SHAFT STEEL SHALL CONFORM w/
ASTM A572 GR. 65, U.N.O.
2. STEEL FOR BASEPLATES UP TO 2" THICK SHALL CONFORM
W/ ASTM A572 GR. 55.  STEEL FOR BASEPLATES OVER 2"
THICK SHALL CONFORM W/ ASTM A572 GR. 50.
3. ALL STEEL PIPE SHALL CONFORM w/ ASTM A53 GR. B (35
KSI), U.N.O.
4. ALL STEEL RECTANGULAR TUBES (HSS) SHALL CONFORM
w/ ASTM A500 GR. B (46 KSI), U.N.O.
5. REINFORCED PORT STEEL SHALL CONFORM w/ ASTM A572
GR. 65, U.N.O.
6. ALL OTHER STEEL SHAPES & PLATES SHALL CONFORM w/
ASTM A36, U.N.O.
7. ALL BOLTS FOR STEEL-TO-STEEL CONNECTIONS SHALL
CONFORM w/ ASTM F3125 GR. A325, U.N.O.
8. ALL ANCHOR BOLTS SHALL CONFORM w/ ASTM A615 GR. 75,
U.N.O.
9. ALL WELDING SHALL BE PERFORMED BY CERTIFIED
WELDERS IN ACCORDANCE w/ THE LATEST VERSION OF THE

ACCORDANCE w/ ASTM A123 AND ASTM F2329 STANDARDS.
11. ALL BOLTED CONNECTIONS SHALL BE TIGHTENED PER THE
"TURN-OF-NUT" METHOD AS DEFINED BY AISC.

GENERAL DESIGN NOTES:
1. FOUNDATION DESIGN IS BASED UPON THE 2018 EDITION INTERNATIONAL BUILDING CODE AND ALL APPLICABLE LOCAL AMENDMENTS.
DESIGN LOADS:
1. THIS FOUNDATION IS DESIGNED TO SUPPORT THE 105'-0" TALL, MONOPOLE TOWER DESIGNED BY PINNACLE CONSULTING, INC. (JOB NO.
AZ11-173). 
2. FACTORED BASE REACTIONS PROVIDED BY TOWER DRAWINGS ARE:
a. MOMENT = 2003 FT-KIPS (1.0 WIND)
b. SHEAR = 24 KIPS (1.0 WIND)
c. AXIAL = 26 KIPS (1.2 DEAD)
FOUNDATION NOTES:
1. FOUNDATION IS DESIGNED UTILIZING TIA-222 REV. H ANNEX F PRESUMPTIVE SOIL PARAMETERS.
2. ASSUMED SAND TYPE SOIL WITH γ=110 pcf,  φ=30°, and k = 35 pci PER TABLE F-1 OF TIA. VERIFY SOIL TYPE DURING CONSTRUCTION.
3. FOUNDATION DESIGN DOES NOT APPLY TO SOILS WITH WEAKER CHARACTERISTICS THAN NOTED ABOVE OR IN LOCATIONS WHERE
UNCOMPACTED, UNDOCUMENTED OR ORGANIC FILL EXIST.  IT IS THE OWNER AND CONTRACTOR'S RESPONSIBILITY TO VERIFY SOIL CONDITIONS
MEET OR EXCEED ASSUMPTIONS NOTED HEREIN.
MATERIAL NOTES:
1. ANCHOR BOLTS:  ASTM A615, GRADE 75
2. STEEL PLATE (TEMPLATES):  ASTM A36 (OR BETTER) UNLESS NOTED OTHERWISE
3. CONCRETE:
a. 28 DAY COMPRESSIVE STRENGTH (f'c) OF 4000 PSI
b. TYPE II PORTLAND CEMENT
c. 3/4" MAXIMUM AGGREGATE SIZE
4. REINFORCING STEEL:  ASTM A615, GRADE 60
FOUNDATION SPECIAL INSPECTIONS:
1. SPECIAL INSPECTIONS ARE REQUIRED PER IBC CHAPTER 17 FOR THE FOLLOWING ITEMS:
a. PERIODIC INSPECTION OF REINFORCING STEEL TO VERIFY SIZE AND PLACEMENT.
b. PERIODIC INSPECTION OF ANCHOR BOLTS TO VERIFY SIZE AND PLACEMENT.
c. CONTINUOUS INSPECTION OF CONCRETE PLACEMENT TO VERIFY PROPER PLACEMENT TECHNIQUES.
GENERAL CONSTRUCTION NOTES:
1. CONCRETE CONSTRUCTION SHALL BE IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE LATEST REVISION OF ACI 301, "SPECIFICATIONS FOR STRUCTURAL
CONCRETE" AND WITH THE LATEST REVISION OF ACI-318, "BUILDING CODE REQUIREMENTS FOR REINFORCED CONCRETE".
1451 McHOOD RD.

RE: Special Use Permit Application for Wireless Facility Project for “AZ11-173 Huntress / AZ3 Winslow 
West 5 Mtn Rd”  
 
Dear
 Navajo County Planning & Development Services Department, 
 
Per your new code regarding Wireless Communication Facilities, special use permit applicants shall submit as  
part of the zoning submittal, “Certification that the wireless communication facility, as represented in the  
application, will comply with all FAA, FCC and other applicable regulations”. 
 
Due to prolonged lead times to obtain environmental services that produce the FAA/FCC approval (roughly 60- 
120 days), we cannot produce that certification as of today, and we are asking for relief from this submittal item 
for the initial submittal. Please note that Sun State Towers has submitted for FAA & FCC review and 
determination of approval via its consultant Trileaf. We expect to have their respective approvals in 60-120 
days, and we will relay to Navajo County upon receipt and/or prior to hearing schedule. We also follow  
FAA/FCC rules and regulations when engineering our projects, and below is an additional TOWAIR  
determination results for the interim time. 
 
 
 
Best regards, 
 
________________________________________ 
Graham Chapman, Site Acquisition Specialist 
480-664-9588 ext. 255

Dear
 Navajo County Planning & Development Services Department, 
 
Per your new code regarding Wireless Communication Facilities, special use permit applicants shall submit as  
part of the zoning submittal, “Certification of whether the applicant is applying for collocation treatment, and  
how many carriers could be accommodated on the facility with adequate signal coverage” 
 
 
 
This document will serve as our confirmation that this Sun State Towers facility will not only accommodate 
Verizon Wireless equipment as the anchor carrier at a 100’ RAD center, but will also accommodate co-location 
requests from at least two (2) future carriers at centerlines of 90’ and 80’, as referenced in the diagram above

Dear Neighbor,   
 
You are receiving this letter because according to the Navajo County Assessors records, you are a 
property owner located in the area surrounding a property that will be home to a new Wireless 
Communication Facility.  
 
This proposed facility is needed to help fill a gap in wireless coverage in the area and will allow for 
increased access to advanced wireless voice and data service.  
 
Pinnacle Consulting, Inc. will hold a neighborhood meeting online via Zoom on Thursday, September 
14th
 at 6pm to 7pm, for the purpose of providing information concerning the proposed construction of a 
new Wireless Communication Facility.

Federal T elecommunications Act of 1996
This is a federal law requiring jurisdictions to authorize requests for 
wireless facilities in a “reasonable period of time”
The jurisdiction (Navajo County) has 150-days to process our application 
once submitted
If they fail to act within that time-frame, we can file suit for “failure to act” 
under section 332(c)(7)(B)(v) within 30-days after that limit has been 
reached

October 5th, 2023  
 
RE: Proposed Wireless Telecommunications Facility (Project Name: AZ11-173 Huntress)  
       Near the cross streets of McHood Road   and   North Park Drive  
       Latitude 35.0749388°, Longitude -110.6808136°  
       Report # 2023-0013343 
 
Hello,  
 
You are receiving this letter because, according to the Navajo County GIS maps, you are a police 
department, fire department, public safety department, water department, or local government with 
jurisdiction within 5 miles of a property that will be home to a new wireless communication facility.  
 
Sun State Towers proposes to place a 105’ wireless communication tower at parcel 103-23-010A (1454 
McHood Rd, Winslow, AZ 86047) that will help fill a gap in wireless coverage and capacity in the area 
and will allow for increased access to advanced wireless voice and data service. Sun State Towers will be 
pursuing a Special Use Permit to place the facility there, and per Navajo County’s new zoning ordinance 
regulations governing applications for wireless communication facilities, Navajo County would like us to 
provide:  
 
“Certification, or other appropriate documentation, that policing, fire departments, public safety, water, 
and local governments having jurisdiction within five (5) miles of the site have been notified of the 
application”  
 
 
If you have questions concerning this facility, please contact me at the contact information below. 
Otherwise, please respond to the email and state that you have no concerns.  
 
Sincerely,  
 
Graham Chapman  
Site Acquisition Specialist at Pinnacle Consulting, Inc.  
1426 N Marvin St, Suite 101  
Gilbert, AZ 85233  
O: (480) 664-9588 ext. 255  
E: graham.chapman@pinnacleco.net

RE: Special Use Permit Application for Wireless Facility Project for “AZ11-173 Huntress / AZ3 Winslow

Dear
 Navajo County Planning & Development Services Department, 
 
Per your new code regarding Wireless Communication Facilities, special use permit applicants shall submit as  
part of the zoning submittal, “Certification that no Navajo County or municipal owned site, or existing wireless 
facility reasonably meets the needs of the applicant, listing all such sites within five (5) miles of the proposed 
site and the reason each is not physically adequate for reasonable commercial coverage, or not economically 
feasible for location” 
 
 
 
The image above is a search ring of all wireless facility sites within a 5-mile radius of our proposed location. As 
you can see, there are only 4 other facilities listed in the search ring, but these facilities already have Verizon 
Wireless equipment on them to serve the c needs of the southern, more-developed areas of Winslow. Adding 
more equipment on those other towers would not adequately address the needs of the rural community to the 
north we are proposing to place our tower in.  
 
Best regards, 
 
________________________________________ 
Graham Chapman, Site Acquisition Specialist 
480-664-9588 ext. 255

PROJECT NARRATIVE

<!-- page 293 -->
Reasons for the Propose Special Use Permit & Benefits to the Area 
 
We are proposing to place a wireless telecommunication facility monopole at parcel 103-23-010A in Winslow 
because we want to provide better cellular coverage and capacity to mobile users in the surrounding area.  
 
Currently, there is a severe lack of service in the northern side of Winslow around the proposed site, and mobile 
phones are reporting low-quality connections for both indoor and outdoor service. This is critical to fix, because 
96% of Americans own a cellular phone and 57% of American homes rely exclusively on cellular phones.  
 
In addition, there are homes in this northern area that do not have the benefit of being located closer to the cell 
towers in southern Winslow. When 84% or more of 9-1-1 emergency calls are made from wireless devices, it is 
a critical issue to fix, especially for homeowners without this infrastructure.   
 
If we place our facility at the requested parcel, we can fill in a huge gap of cellular coverage and capacity that 
the community has been dealing with for a long time. On the following page are coverage maps prepared by a 
registered radio-frequency engineer that show the current coverage in the area and the proposed increased 
coverage if we are able to place our facility.

<!-- page 295 -->
Explanation of Proposed Use 
 
Our proposal is to place a facility in the empty undeveloped land of 1454 McHood Road, Winslow, AZ 86047 
in a 40’x40’ ground-space area. However, per the landlrod’s request, we are also going to be placing a fence 
running north-south alonmg the property the same distance away from the falling radius of the tower to ensure 
that our operations are separated from the landlord’s activities. While we originally proposed a stealth deisgn, 
given the lack of trees or development in the area, and given comments in the pre-application meeting, we are 
going to propose a traditional monopole tower.  
 
Our exact scope of work is as follows: 
 
- Install proposed 105’-0” Sun State Towers monopole 
- Install proposed 40’x40’x8’-0” CMU wall 
- Install proposed outdoor power cabinet 
- Install proposed outdoor RF cabinet 
- Install proposed 50kW diesel generator 
- Install proposed electrical service 
- Install proposed telco service 
- Install [12] proposed antennas 
- Install [12] proposed remote radio heads 
- Install [1] proposed OVP-12 
- Install [2] proposed hybrid cables 
- Install proposed chain-link fence 
- Install proposed access swing gate 
 
On the following page are images from our site plan that illustrate and explain our proposed use on a more 
technical and visual level.

<!-- page 297 -->
Schedule of Development 
 
 
 
While different projects and different scopes may require slightly altered construction schedules, above is a 
general development schedule that all Sun State Tower projects generally follow. 
Once zoning submittal is achieved, we will follow the Special Use Permit process until the SUP is granted. 
Once that is granted, we move onto building permit submittal. Once that building permit is granted, we then 
move onto actual construction. 
Actual construction will generally follow the activity table above. Typically, construction of the Sun State 
Tower will take approximately one month, and then construction and installation of the Verizon equipment on 
the tower takes another month. So in total, our tower construction time from start to finish is about 2 months.

<!-- page 298 -->
Compatability of Proposed Use with Adjacent Uses & Character of the Area 
 
Our proposed parcel (103-23-010A) is currently an empty, undeveloped parcel, and the property is zoned as “A-
General” but assessed as non-residential. Many of the surrounding properties in the vicinity are also all zoned as 
A-General but serve and are assessed as homes with large square footage and large open spaces. 
The purpose of A-General zoning is “to provide for all the unincorporated area of the County . . . and . . . Uses 
permitted in the "A-General Zone", include farm and non-farm residential uses, farms, recreational, 
institutional, commercial and industrial uses as specifically listed in this Article. Other uses may be permitted 
as Special Uses under Article 20.” 
While our use will require a Special Use Permit, it is going to be a commercial use that would be allowed in the 
A-General zoning designation. It is also a use that will benefit all the other allowable uses in that area (farm, 
non-farm, recreational, institutional, commercial, etc.) because wireless capacity helps all of those uses in 
different ways. Furthermore, even though it is a rural area, the lack of trees or woody areas being a part of the 
character of the area make our proposal for a traditional monopole a rational proposal.

Dear William Bess,  
The purpose of this letter is to provide a written engineering narrative confirming the structural stability 
of the proposed monopole tower, foundation and antenna mounti ng attachments at the subject site. 
The proposed monopole tower, foundation and antenna mounts were designed in accordance with the 
2018  International  Building  Code  with  Navajo  County  Amendments,  ASCE  7‐16  and  the  adopted  by 
reference standard ANSI/TIA‐222‐H, Structural Standard for Antenna Supporting Structures and Antennas.  
The following structural de sign criteria was used for the proposed tower, foundation, and antenna mount 
design: 
 Wind Speed: 115 mph (Ultimate)
 Service Wind Speed: 60 mph (Ultimate – Deflection limits only)
 Risk Category II
 Exposure Category C
 Topographic Category 1
 Ice: 0.25” w/ 30 mph wind gust speed (I gnored per TIA‐222‐H 2.6.4)
 Seismic Design Category B (Ss = 0.233 and S1 = 0.073)
The proposed 105’ monopole tower and foundation were designed to support carrier antenna arrays at 
the 100’, 90’ and 80’ elevations.  The 100’ elevation will be for Verizon and the design loading will include 
(12) panel antennas, (12) radios and a double T‐Arm style mount with pipe stiffeners.   The 90’ and 80’
elevations will incl
ude assumed future loading similar to the Verizon antenna array.  The foundation was
designed based on presumptive soil parameters as defined in Table F‐1 of the ANSI/TIA‐222‐H standard.
After zoning approval has been granted, a full geotechnical investigation will be performed, an d  the final
foundation design will be reviewed and revised as needed based on the site‐specific soil strength values.
Please reference the tower and foundation design calcs and drawings prepared by Pinnacle Consulting,
Inc., dated October 16, 2023, for more information regarding the analysis methods and capacities.

General Notes
1. The  contractor and manufacturer shall verify all dimensions prior to fabrication
or construction and notify engineer of any noted discrepancy.
2. Typical  details apply unless noted otherwise.  
3. Drawings  and calculations represent the finished conditions upon completion
of construction.  Contractor is responsible for means and methods including 
rigging, shoring, temporary bracing, etc. as required to complete construction.
4. Drawings  and calculations apply only to members specifically designed herein.  
Structural Steel
1. Structural  Steel shall conform to the following ASTM specifications:
Tapered Poles: A572,  Grade 65
Baseplates (Up to 2" thick): A572,  Grade 55
Baseplates (Over 2" thick): A572,  Grade 50
Pipe: A53,  Grade B (35 ksi)
Hollow Structural Sections: A500,  Grade B (46 ksi)
Plate for Reinforced Ports: A572,  Grade 65
All Other Shapes and Plates: A36
Bolts (High Strength): F3125,  Grade A325
Anchor Bolts: A615, Grade 75 (as noted)
F1554, Grade 55 
2. High  strength bolts shall be fully tensioned by turn‐of‐nut or 
other AISC approved method.
3. Welding  shall be performed by welders certified in the process and position required
in accordance with AWS D1.1 using 70 ksi electrodes, unless noted otherwise.
4. All  steel shall be hot dipped galvanized in accordance with ASTM A123.

  Tower Input Data    
 
 
The tower is a monopole. 
This tower is designed using the TIA-222-H standard. 
The following design criteria apply:  
 Tower base elevation above sea level: 4831.14 ft. 
 Basic wind speed of 115 mph. 
 Risk Category II. 
 Exposure Category C. 
 Simplified Topographic Factor Procedure for wind speed-up calculations is used. 
 Topographic Category: 1. 
 Crest Height: 0.00 ft. 
 Deflections calculated using a wind speed of 60 mph. 
 A non-linear (P-delta) analysis was used. 
 Pressures are calculated at each section. 
 Stress ratio used in pole design is 1. 
 Local bending stresses due to climbing loads, feed line supports, and appurtenance mounts are not considered.

Definitions of Run Messages and Notes:
‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐‐
  C = 
concrete in section has cracked in tension.
  Y = stress in reinforcing steel has reached yield stress.
  T = ACI 318 criteria for tension‐controlled section met, tensile strain in 
      reinforcement exceeds 0.005 while simultaneously compressive strain in 
      concrete more than 0.003. See ACI 318‐14, Section 21.2.3.
  Z = depth of tensile zone in concrete section is less than 10 percent of 
      section depth.
Bending Stiffness (EI) = Computed Bending Moment / Curvature.
Position of neutral axis is measured from edge of compression side of pile.
Compressive stresses and strains are positive in sign.
Tensile stresses and strains are negative in sign.

In ACI 318, the value of the strength reduction factor depends on whether

or the value required by the design standard being followed.
